<a id="f561"></a>
### #561 · unconfirmed

**Where:** 0x0130a858 — dw4asset render --scene cam.tsv (crate attr 12676, texdir proptex/g_woodencasez01.rgba)

The crate's remaining difference from the real frame is the port's invented per-mesh tint, not the texture: the pack decode is exact, so the crate's *material* is the open item

**Notes:** Isolated the crate from the camera-matched Goblin Pass frame exactly: rendered the scene with the crate's TSV row and without it, so the mask (6415 px, bbox x 177..277, y 108..206) is the crate's own coverage, and measured real vs port on that mask. Per-mesh tint recovered exactly by dividing the tinted render by the --white-tint one: (0.591,0.508,0.489), std <=0.03 per channel -- a uniform per-mesh multiply, as expected of the hash palette; it darkens the crate by about half. Means on the mask: real (88.5,71.8,53.5), port with tint (56.2,42.3,26.2), port with --white-tint (94.2,82.5,53.1) -- so *no* tint is much closer in level (the tint, not the texture, is what makes the port's crate read dark). Two residuals are visible in those numbers, and the first reading of them was partly wrong -- corrected by #562, which see: (1) the sampler was NEAREST while the packs' own materials ask for linear (#562 fixes it); but this entry originally claimed the crate's saturation gap was that filtering, at "~10x magnification", and neither half holds -- at the camera-matched distance the crate is about 1:1 texel per pixel, so #562 moves its box mean by 0.1 (42.68 -> 42.24 mean abs difference over the mask), and the 41.4-vs-59.6 saturation comparison was confounded by comparing the real frame against the *untinted* render, whose saturation is only that high because the missing tint is not darkening it: the same render tinted reads 32.7, the real 41.4. So filtering is not the crate's residual and the saturation figure is not evidence about it; what the untinted-vs-real ratio does still show is (2) the rig's blue deficit -- real/untinted median (0.896,0.794,1.434): the real crate is *paler and bluer* than the atlas alone can produce, and the wall shows the same blue deficit (real wall blue 41.2 against the port's 26.7), which a per-channel multiply cannot fix. The tint recovery and the box means above are unaffected. The atlas, the 415x8-byte UV stream and the image index block are byte-identical to live memory (#558), so neither (1) nor (2) is a texture defect. The crate is the best case for recovering the real material colours (#485): it is large, flat-lit, and its tint can be recovered exactly by the two-render division above; fitting a single gain from the untinted render to the real frame leaves a large residual (mean abs 41.9/255) because the correction is not a gain. Folded into #562, the current answer for Texture filtering and the crate's remaining difference; this finding is kept for its evidence and history.

**Evidence:** Masked comparison of the crate's coverage in the camera-matched Goblin Pass frame against the real frame

<a id="f562"></a>
### #562 · confirmed

**Where:** igTextureAttr._magFilter/_minFilter/_mipmapMode (reflection: +0x14/+0x18/+0x28); port/src/render.c raster_tri texture fetch, src/gl_render.c glTexParameteri

The game filters its textures linearly (GS bilinear on mag and min, no mipmaps) and the port sampled them nearest; the game's own igTextureAttr records carry the evidence

**Notes:** Decisive evidence in the data, not a guess: the stage packs' own material sets say so. In NDMW.AFS:614 and:621 there are 323 igTextureBindAttr-backed igTextureAttr records; 300 of them carry (_magFilter,_minFilter,_mipmapMode) = (1,1,0) -- linear magnification, linear minification, no mipmaps -- and the 23 that carry (0,0,0) are the placeholder records 'PromotedAttr' (21) and 'WN04' (2), so every one of the 114 distinct *named* materials asks for linear. The crate's own material g_woodencasez01 is (1,1,0), as are g_bbarrelsz01, the floor and the big terrain meshes. _wrapS/_wrapT are 1 on all of them, and the port already clamps, which is the same 0/1 ordering (0 = repeat, 1 = clamp), so 1 is the non-default mode in both fields. igTextureAttr has no other sampling field, and these four are exactly the GS's TEX0 CLAMP and TEX1 MMAG/MMIN/MXL bits. The enum's member names could not be read (the reflection descriptors' desc pointers, e.g. 0x55EF70, point past the ELF's single segment and are zero in a live 32 MB dump), so the mapping rests on the GS bit layout plus the measurement. Measured on the camera-matched Goblin Pass frame against the real capture: whole-frame mean abs difference 28.86 -> 27.09, linear closer on 57.2% of pixels, mean per-pixel improvement 1.77, and no 32x32 block where nearest is clearly better (worst -0.1); the floor box (260,330)-(420,420) moves (67.9,47.0,31.0) -> (68.1,47.1,31.1) toward the real (69.7,47.5,30.6); the wall box (120,60)-(250,110) barely moves ((33.5,32.3,26.7) -> (33.4,32.1,26.3) against the real (40.9,36.5,41.2)) because its error is the material guess, not the sampler. The effect is largest where the texture is magnified -- the floor/rock in front of the camera, which is the capture -- and is what makes the real frame's soft look. IMPORTANT: this does NOT explain the crate's darkness, because at this camera distance the crate is roughly 1:1 texel:pixel (its mask's mean abs difference 42.68 -> 42.24, its box mean (56.2,42.3,26.2) -> (56.3,42.5,26.3) against the real (88.5,71.8,53.5)); that residual is the port's invented per-mesh tint (#561). Topic head for Texture filtering and the crate's remaining difference: also read #561 (folded in, kept for their evidence).

**Evidence:** port/src/render.c

<a id="f563"></a>
### #563 · confirmed

**Where:** 0x01381f60 — beTextureCtrlInfo._name -> beTextureCtrlAttr._attrSet -> igAttrSet (+0x20 _attributes); scripts/live_materials.py; port/src/render.c light_term/raster_tri

The world materials are live-built attr sets that are unlit, alpha-blended, depth-write-off and texture-matrix-on, and carry no material colour; the port's hash tint stands in for a state difference, not a colour

**Notes:** The world materials are built at LOAD TIME and are not in the pack, and what they carry is state, not colour. 90 material sets live in the Goblin Pass dump behind beTextureCtrlInfo._name -> beTextureCtrlAttr._attrSet (scripts/live_materials.py --from \<dump> --dump reads them all; the walk is texture_map.py's, extended past the texture bind to every attribute). The two that cover most of this frame say:
 BGGround00n (ground and its rock walls): igBlendStateAttr._enabled=1, igBlendFunctionAttr _src=4 _dst=5, igCullFaceAttr._enabled=0, igDepthWriteStateAttr._enabled=0, igLightingStateAttr._enabled=0, igTextureMatrixStateAttr._enabled=1, 3x igTextureBindAttr
 BGObj00n (world objects): the same except _src=4 _dst=1, plus 20 texture binds
 BGGround00s / BGObj00s / wall01z01: a texture bind and nothing else (so they inherit the defaults)
So the geometry the port lights is drawn UNLIT by the game (igLightingStateAttr._enabled = 0 -- the texture times a white vertex colour, no LIGHT program), with alpha blending on, depth write off, culling off and a texture matrix enabled. There is NO material colour to recover: neither set carries an igMaterialAttr or igColorAttr, and the earlier enumeration of every pack attr set (finding #557's work) found those classes only on the scene-level sets (sceneGraph = 0.4 grey, WN00-WN03/EF02/GM01/GM03 = white, all with lighting state 0, i.e. unlit UI/background/effect draws). The port's per-mesh hash tint is therefore standing in for a state difference that does not exist as a colour, and should eventually be deleted rather than matched.

Measured, camera-matched Goblin Pass frame vs the real capture (whole-frame mean abs difference; floor box (260,330)-(420,420); wall box (120,60)-(250,110); real floor (69.7,47.5,30.6), real wall (40.9,36.5,41.2)):
 lit + hash tint (today's default): 27.09, floor (68.1,47.1,31.1), wall (33.4,32.1,26.3)
 everything unlit (--white-tint --ambient 1,1,1 --lights /dev/null): 32.38, floor (75.4,53.6,31.1), wall (59.5,66.6,49.1)
 the packs' own rig (all 3 igLightAttr records, ambient 0.5,0.5,0.55): 26.01, floor (74.5,52.6,28.9), wall (21.3,20.5,14.1)
 the live rig (#564): 28.23; live ambient only, built-in key + spot: 26.40
Unlit is RIGHT for the floor (75.4,53.6,31.1 against the real 69.7,47.5,30.6 -- closer than the lit 68.1,47.1,31.1 in R and G) and wrong for the wall (59.5,66.6,49.1), which is too bright AND too green once unlit while the lit frame is too dark and too red -- i.e. the wall's error is the sampled texel, not the light, which is the texture-matrix/UV question (#547), and the same attrs enable the texture matrix. So a half-fix (unlit without the UV work) regresses the frame metric, which is why nothing was changed in the port beyond the comment that claimed its rig was the packs'.

Per-mesh solve (each mesh's own mask from the whole-scene-minus-that-row render, then the port's tint recovered exactly by dividing the tinted render by --white-tint, and the implied light_real/light_port = tint * real/port):
 terrain mesh P11G00s: tint (0.641,0.596,0.610), real/port (1.21,1.18,1.30) -> implied (0.773,0.703,0.795)
 player P_susanoo01: the same mask and numbers (the player stands in front of that shell)
 barrel g_bbarrelsz01: tint (0.647,0.603,0.561), real/port (1.34,1.34,1.62) -> implied (0.864,0.810,0.911)
 crate g_woodencasez01: tint (0.591,0.509,0.456), real/port (1.57,1.69,2.03) -> implied (0.929,0.860,0.928)
So the correction the port needs is per-mesh and not a colour: a common green-suppressing, blue-preserving shape at a per-mesh level (0.77 / 0.86 / 0.93) -- exactly what an unlit texture blended over the stage's backdrop at different alpha would look like, and the reason no per-mesh tint can be right. The masks for rows 0-14 (the big ground shells) are unusable this way: they occlude everything, so removing one changes a third of the frame.

**Evidence:** scripts/live_materials.py; port/src/render.c

<a id="f564"></a>
### #564 · refuted

**Where:** 0x0056AF40 — p2lighting runtime globals 0x56AF40/0x56AF94, descriptors 0x6e95b0/0x6e9650; port/src/render.c builtin_rig; live_rig.py

The p2lighting rig the game runs is two lights -- ambient (0.25,0.20,0.35) and one directional (1.20,1.15,1.20) along (0.3214,-0.866,0.383) -- readable only mid-render, and its colours are not in the disc

**Notes:** REFUTED by #580 on the Goblin Pass frame: the world draws (P11G00n floor/wall/terrain) carry per-vertex gouraud colours in the SAME range as the lit props (R31..127 G25..127 B44..127), i.e. they ARE lit by the live rig, not unlit with pre-shaded textures. The live material dump also has only 2 igAttrSet records carrying igLightingStateAttr (both _enabled=0 -- the overlay materials), not BGGround00n/BGObj00n; the 'world unlit' conclusion was over-read from the absence of a lighting attr on the sets this dump does list. The 0.5 vertex colours are the GS's modulate convention, not a pre-shaded texture. Folded into #580, the current answer for World is lit by the same rig as props; this finding is kept for its evidence and history.

**Evidence:** Running-game read of the p2lighting rig mid-render

<a id="f565"></a>
### #565 · confirmed

**Where:** 0x01381f60 — beTextureCtrlAttr._attrSet -> igAttrSet +0x1c shape list; port/src/dw4asset.c scene TSV texture column (first name only)

The world meshes' texture is a per-shape binding in the live material records (BGGround00n = P11G00n x5 shapes + P10G00n x1 + P10G02n x1), and applying one texture per material row is what makes the world geometry read wrong

**Notes:** The world meshes' texture is a PER-SHAPE binding inside the live material records, and the port applies one texture per material row, which is why the world geometry reads wrong. Recovered from the Goblin Pass dump (TextureMap walk, one beTextureCtrlAttr per material; the shape list is the attr set's +0x1c igNodeList):
 BGGround00n: P11G00n.png x5 shapes, P10G00n.png x1, P10G02n.png x1
 BGGround00s: P11G00s.png x4 shapes, P10G00s.png x1
 BGObj00n: 15 records, 1-6 shapes each (FD01BJ03, P11G00n, Z1BJ01LM, TA01BJ20, TA01BJ04 x5, TA01BJ02, sfZ0SY10, TA01BJ01, Z1BJ0600 x6, TA01BJ18 x2, TA02BJ01, FD02BJ07 x2, FD01BJ07 x3, FD01BJ06 x3, FD01BJ05 x2)
 wall01z01: TA01GM02.png x5 shapes
So the big terrain meshes are split across three different atlases, and P11G00n and P10G00n are NOT variants of one atlas: rendered side by side against the real frame, P11G00n is a bright green-grey mossy rock and P10G00n a dark grey rock (both carry the blue light-dash band). Finding #547's conclusion ("P11G00n is the wall's texture, and it reproduces the band") was a by-eye call from the material's texture SET (the info-level union has all three names, preferred P11G00n first); region by region the two do not agree.

Measured, camera-matched Goblin Pass frame, port vs the real capture (whole-frame mean abs difference; upper wall box (120,60)-(250,110); right-hand region box (430,60)-(600,180); floor box (260,330)-(420,420); real upper wall (40.9,36.5,41.2), real right-hand region (67.2,62.5,61.5), real floor (69.7,47.5,30.6)):
 lit + hash tint, P11G00n everywhere (today's default): 27.09, upper (33.4,32.1,26.3), right (33.5,28.9,22.1), floor (68.1,47.1,31.1)
 lit + hash tint, P10G00n everywhere: 27.48, upper (27.1,22.9,30.5), right (27.8,22.9,26.1), floor (68.1,47.1,31.1)
 unlit, P11G00n everywhere: 32.38, upper (59.5,66.6,49.1), right (64.3,64.7,46.1), floor (75.4,53.6,31.1)
 unlit, P10G00n everywhere: 31.51, upper (48.9,47.7,56.9), right (52.1,49.1,53.0), floor (75.4,53.6,31.1)
The two regions disagree in opposite directions: the RIGHT-hand region matches P11G00n (unlit right (64.3,64.7,46.1) against the real (67.2,62.5,61.5)) and the UPPER wall matches P10G00n (unlit upper (48.9,47.7,56.9) against the real (40.9,36.5,41.2), and it is the only candidate whose blue exceeds green, which the real's also does). One texture per row therefore cannot be right, and no single-texture swap improves the frame mean: the fix is the per-shape assignment. The floor is unaffected by either (rows 16-19 are the `s` material, P11G00s first) and its unlit box (75.4,53.6,31.1) is already within ~8% of the real (69.7,47.5,30.6).

FOLLOW-UP (finding #566, measured): the map above is not the whole story -- rendering the frame with one row's texture swapped at a time shows that only pack 21835 is visible in this frame (114,809 px, 41 %, and 99.8 % of the wall box), every other row changing ZERO pixels, and 21835's recovered texture is P11G00n.png -- the same one the port already uses -- while the real frame's world matches the P10G00n atlas (dark band of blue dashes on grey rock) instead. So the material's texture set does not by itself explain that region's colour, and the wall's remaining error is either a bad prim-count key for streamed meshes or the missing unlit/alpha-blend/texture-matrix state. See #566 for the numbers. Why it is not wired yet: the link from a live geometry shape to the pack geometry-attribute id is what the port needs (it takes the first name of the TSV's texture column), and for these meshes `camera_match`'s vertex-buffer content match does not reach them -- a2p has NO entry for the seven BGGround00n geometry attrs (0x1381ef8, 0x1381ea8, 0x11960c0, 0x1195cd0, 0x1380068, 0x1196070, 0x137fcd8), so the TSV's texture for those rows came from the info-level fallback (which unions all three names and prefers P11G00n), not from the shape. The candidate link is (numVerts, bbox) matching against the pack's attrs -- the live geoms' addresses are not objects with vtables at the addresses the shape list gave (the values there are vertex floats), so the geometry must be reached through the shape's own attr list, not by the address the walk returned.

**Evidence:** scripts/texture_map.py; port/src/dw4asset.c

<a id="f566"></a>
### #566 · unconfirmed

**Where:** 0x01382c00 — beTextureCtrlAttr -> igAttrSet +0x1c shape list -> igGeometry -> igGeometryAttr1_5 (+0x14 _primType, +0x18 _numPrims); port scene TSV texture column

The world meshes' texture can be recovered per shape by matching the live material records' (primType, numPrims) to the pack attributes -- but for the one mesh this frame actually draws the recovered texture (P11G00n) does not reproduce the real world's look, so the key or the material state is still wrong

**Notes:** Method: the world material records list their geometry shapes (beTextureCtrlAttr -> attr set -> +0x1c shape list -> igGeometry), and every shape carries a (primType, numPrims) pair that can be matched against the pack attributes WITHOUT the vertex-buffer content match that camera_match uses (that match does not reach these meshes: a2p has no entry for any of the BGGround00n geometry attrs). Building the map that way gives, for the 15 "n" rows of the camera-matched TSV:
 111 -> TA02BJ01.png, 1229 -> TA01GM02.png, 3190 -> P11G00n.png, 3192 -> P10G00n.png, 3194 -> P11G00n.png,
 7521 -> TA01BJ20.png, 21721 -> P10G02n.png, 21732 -> P11G00n.png, 21833 -> P11G00n.png, 21835 -> P11G00n.png,
 8533 / 41789 / 49587 -> P11G00s.png, 41757 -> TA02BJ01.png, 8391 -> P10G00s.png; and the remaining rows are ambiguous because several live shapes share a prim count (7528: Z1BJ01LM/TA01BJ04; 7851: P11G00n x5 / TA01GM02 x4; 12217 and 31788: P11G00s / TA01BJ01).
Which rows matter was then measured, not assumed: rendering the frame with ONE row's texture swapped to a distinctive atlas (P10G02n, near-white) shows that in this frame only ONE row is visible at all -- pack 21835, whose swap changes 114,809 px (41 % of the frame) and 99.8 % of the "wall" box. Every other row of 111/1229/3190/3192/3194/7521/7528/7851/8391/8533/12217/21721/21732/21833 changes ZERO pixels: the ground and floor of this camera-matched frame are drawn by that one screen-filling mesh. So sub-texture differences on the other rows cannot explain anything here, and the (primType, numPrims) map's ambiguities do not affect this frame. Pack 21835 is (primType 4, numPrims 199, numVerts 2038) and it is the ONLY attr in pack 614 with 199 prims, so its live counterpart is the BGGround00n shape with 199 prims, whose record binds P11G00n.png -- the same texture the port already uses (the fallback column's preferred first name). The port's texture choice for the visible mesh is therefore CORRECT.

The conflict that remains, stated plainly: with that (recovered) texture the port's world does not look like the real one -- unlit it is (59.5,66.6,49.1) in the wall box against the real (40.9,36.5,41.2), and the real's band of blue light dashes is a dark strip while the port's is bright -- whereas the material's P10G00n atlas reproduces the real's dark band and grey rock (unlit (48.9,47.7,56.9), and it is the only candidate whose blue exceeds green, as the real's does). Two explanations, neither verified: (a) the prim-count key is not sound for these meshes -- the engine streams/repacks them, so a live _numPrims may not equal the pack's (the live vertex arrays are drained -- _vdata 0 at +0x08, numVerts 0 at +0x0c -- so numVerts/bbox cannot be used as a second key, and the pack's 199-prim attr would then not be the live 199-prim shape); or (b) the binding is right and the whole difference is the missing unlit/alpha-blend state plus the *texture matrix* the same material enables (igTextureMatrixStateAttr._enabled=1), which would have to darken the band and shift the hue (#563/#547). The measurement that would separate them is the per-draw material (the staged packet path, #522) or a second geometric key that survives streaming. Folded into #570, the current answer for Texture of the visible world mesh 21835; this finding is kept for its evidence and history.

**Evidence:** scripts/texture_map.py

<a id="f567"></a>
### #567 · confirmed

**Where:** 0x00522980 — igTextureMatrixAttr._m (+0x0c) / _isProjective (+0x52); igTextureMatrixStateAttr._enabled; port/src/render.c UV affine, src/dw4asset.c --uv-offset/--uv-scale

The staged GS packet route is closed offline (no command stream in EE RAM, no VU1 via PINE); the engine's texture matrix is a UV offset that the ground material enables but does not carry; the world's enabled blend is opaque; and a global UV affine does not fix the tile

**Notes:** Three results, two of them negatives that close off routes, and one measured limit.

(1) THE GS COMMAND STREAM IS NOT IN EE RAM, and PINE cannot reach where it is. #522 says the region 0x00cc3000-0x00d3xxxx holds "the environment vertex data verbatim plus 16-byte command records". A scan of that whole region -- and then of all 32 MB -- for GIF tags (bits 16..45 must be zero; NREG, NLOOP, EOP, PRE, PRIM, FLG then read out) finds no A+D register list at all: the 136 REGLIST-shaped hits in 32 MB are MIPS code that happens to satisfy the tag mask (they decode as daddu/andi sequences), and the named region's first bytes are per-vertex data (a tag byte then floats), not 16-byte records. So either the 16-byte records are elsewhere or (more likely) the packets are produced in the VU1 and exist only transiently; the last-decoded draw state cannot be read out of a dump. The PINE route to the VU1 is closed too: the VU0/VU1 mirrors 0x11000000 and 0x11008000 read as all zeros through pi-pine (only EE RAM is served -- 0x56AF60 reads its known pointer 0x6e9820 in the same session, so the client itself is fine). The practical route left is a PCSX2 GS dump (.gs): the file is the frame's whole GIF stream, so TEX0/ALPHA/RGBAQ per draw are in it. This container's [Hotkeys] block binds only TogglePause and FrameAdvance, so the dump hotkey has to be seeded first (as pi-emu bind does for pause/step) and a.gs parser written. That is the honest state of the packet path.

(2) THE ENABLED TEXTURE MATRIX IS A UV OFFSET, AND THE GROUND MATERIAL DOES NOT CARRY ONE. 54 live igTextureMatrixAttr instances (vtable 0x522980) exist in the Goblin Pass dump and every one is the identity with a translation in matrix elements 12..13 (_unitID 0, _outputElementCount 0xffff, _isProjective 0): e.g. (-0.1376, 0), (-0.8269, 0), (-0.1271, 0), (0, -0.0769), (+0.25, -0.75) -- so the engine's texture matrix is a UV offset, not a projective transform. But BGGround00n's attr set holds exactly seven attributes -- igBlendStateAttr, igBlendFunctionAttr, igCullFaceAttr, igDepthWriteStateAttr, igLightingStateAttr, igTextureMatrixStateAttr (the *state* enabler) and igTextureBindAttr -- and no matrix, so the matrix comes from elsewhere (the igTextureMatrixAttrPool, a node-level sibling or the draw code). The tile/UV question of #547 is therefore still open, and its answer is one of these offsets, not a different texture.

(3) THE BLEND IS OPAQUE, SO IT DOES NOT EXPLAIN THE WORLD'S LOOK. The ground textures' palettes are constant alpha 0x80 -- live P11G00n 256x128, P10G00n 64x128, P11G00s 128x128 and TA01GM02 64x128 all read alpha exactly 128 with zero variation -- and the GS treats 0x80 as 1.0. With igBlendStateAttr._enabled = 1 (_src 4, _dst 5) the source term is therefore As = 1.0 and the blend reduces to a copy: the world geometry is drawn effectively opaque, so the port needs no blending for it and the blend is not what darkens the wall. (Useful negative: it removes the blend from the list of explanations for the wall's colour, leaving the UV/tile and the vertex colour.)

(4) MEASURED, A GLOBAL UV AFFINE IS NOT THE ANSWER EITHER. Port knobs added for the test (--uv-offset U,V, --uv-scale S; inert by default -- the baseline render is byte-identical to before), unlit renders of the camera-matched frame, wall box (120,60)-(250,110) against the real (40.9,36.5,41.2):
 as authored: wall (59.5,66.6,49.1), box mean abs err 37.51, frame 32.38
 uv scale 2: wall (51.4,53.8,38.1), 24.81, frame 39.32
 uv scale 4: wall (53.8,55.8,40.1), 23.97, frame 46.33
 uv offset (0,+0.5): wall (44.8,49.1,31.5), 26.02, frame 32.46
 uv offset (-0.8269,0): wall (52.2,56.4,29.1), 28.98, frame 33.52
 uv offset (-0.1376,0): wall (50.4,56.1,36.2), 31.22, frame 33.34
 uv offset (+0.25,-0.75): wall (97.2,75.1,83.1), 51.44, frame 38.06
Scaling the UVs 2-4x improves the *wall* a lot but destroys the rest of the frame (the floor box goes (75.4,53.6,31.1) -> (88.9,84.8,88.7) at scale 4), which is exactly what a per-material transform should look like: the ground mesh needs one transform and the terrain/floor it also covers needs another. And none of the offsets actually found in the live matrices improves the wall (the best measured offset, (0,+0.5), is not one of them), so the 54 matrices found are for other objects and the ground material's own matrix is still unlocated. Tile-scale is nonetheless the most promising direction: it is the one change that moves the wall's hue toward the real's neutral-cool (53.8,55.8,40.1 against 40.9,36.5,41.2) instead of the green the authored UVs give.

**Evidence:** port/src/render.c

<a id="f568"></a>
### #568 · confirmed

**Where:** 0x0052e233 — PCSX2.gs dump records (GS/GSDump.cpp); port/src/render.c tint/lighting; scripts/gsdump.py

The PCSX2 GS dump route works and shows the frame draws 43,788 vertices in 3-register PACKED packets (STQ/UV/XYZF2) with no colour register anywhere -- the per-draw colour is a constant, so no per-mesh colour exists in the pipeline

**Notes:** ROUTE, now working. The.gs format is confirmed from PCSX2's own writer (GS/GSDump.cpp): 4-byte fake CRC 0xFFFFFFFF, u32 header_size, a 9-word GSDumpHeader {state_version, state_size, crc, serial_offset, serial_size, screenshot w, h, offset, size}, the serial, the screenshot pixels (640*480*4 = 1,228,800), the freeze data (state_size bytes) and a GSPrivRegSet, then a record stream: transfer = u8 0 + u8 index + u32 size + payload; readfifo = u8 2 + u32 size (qwords); vsync = u8 3 + GSPrivRegSet + u8 1 + u8 field. The GSPrivRegSet size is not in the header; it is found by looking for the first offset whose record chain walks to the end of the file (here 0x2000, records starting at 0x52e233 for this dump). The dump is zstd (a single frame; python-zstandard). Recipe here: add `GSDumpSingleFrame = Keyboard/F8` to [Hotkeys] (unbound in this container), then `pi-input key F8 --activate` with the game running; dumps land in PCSX2's snaps folder as *.gs.zst alongside a PNG of the same frame. Parser: scripts/gsdump.py (walks records, decodes PACKED/REGLIST/IMAGE packets; --head/--rgbaq).

WHAT THE FRAME'S STREAM CONTAINS (Goblin Pass, the crate/barrel state, 4 vsyncs = the 2-3 frames PCSX2 writes): 7,572 records = 7,568 transfers + 4 vsyncs, all with index 3 (one path); 6,988 GIF packets; 43,788 vertices. The packet shapes are dominated by one form -- PACKED (flg=0), NREG=3, PRIM=4/5 (triangle strip/list), NLOOP = the vertex count: 3,572 packets of 3 vertices, 1,300 of 4, 556 of 3, 584 "big" (>=17), plus a tail -- and nothing else draws geometry. Only 128 small state packets exist in the whole frame (60 with NREG=2, 68 with NREG=4, NLOOP=1, PRIM=0), and just 8 single-register packets, whose values are 0x000a0000 / 0x000a008c (not colours).

THE VERTEX FORMAT HAS NO COLOUR REGISTER. A 3-register vertex packet decodes as {STQ, UV, XYZF2}: the first 16-byte chunk of each vertex is three floats (e.g. S=0.0035, T=0.0154, Q=0.0156) -- a texture-coordinate triple, not a colour word (a colour there would be bytes like 47,189,123,59 with alpha 59, which is not a GS alpha value) -- the second chunk is 16-bit UV pairs, the third is the XYZF2 layout (x:16, y:16, z:24, f:8). A colour-carrying vertex would need NREG=4 (RGBAQ + STQ + UV + XYZF2) and there are none in the frame. So the GS multiplies every texel by its *last-set* RGBAQ: the vertex/draw colour is a constant across this frame, not per mesh and not per vertex.

WHY THAT MATTERS: there is no per-mesh colour anywhere in the pipeline -- not in the material attr sets (#563: no igMaterialAttr, and the world materials set igLightingStateAttr._enabled = 0, i.e. unlit), and not in the GS stream (this finding). So the port's per-mesh hash palette stands in for nothing, and the variation the real frame shows across meshes has to come from the *texels* (which atlas and which UVs each mesh samples) and from how many passes a mesh is drawn in -- i.e. the world is drawn unlit with pre-shaded textures, which also explains the wall's dark band being in the atlas. Next: the dump's *state block* is the freeze data (m_env.CTXT[i].TEX0/TEX1/ALPHA/TEST, m_v.RGBAQ,...), so the constant colour and the per-frame texture state can be read out of it, and the 128 state packets carry the per-draw changes once the packed register cycle is tracked from the state's initial index. Folded into #569, the current answer for Goblin Pass vertex packet type and per-vertex colour; this finding is kept for its evidence and history.

**Evidence:** scripts/gsdump.py

<a id="f569"></a>
### #569 · confirmed

**Where:** 0x0052e233 — PCSX2 GSState::GIFPackedRegHandlerSTQRGBAXYZF2; DW4_Port scripts/gsdump.py draws

The Goblin Pass frame's PACKED NREG=3 vertex packets are the STQRGBAXYZF2 complex type (STQ + RGBA + XYZF2) and carry a per-vertex colour -- finding #568's 'no colour register / constant RGBAQ' is refuted

**Notes:** The tag REGS for 6748 of the frame's packets is fffff412; PCSX2's GIFPath::SetTag unpacks it to registers (2,1,4) = STQ,RGBA,XYZF2, which the type detector matches (regs.U32[0]==0x00040102) and routes to GIFPackedRegHandlerSTQRGBAXYZF2. The handler reads r[1]'s low bytes as RGBA. The values are real colours and vary within one strip: the two dominant ones are (31,25,44,128) and (127,127,127,128), a 2:1 pattern in many 3-vertex tags. (31,25,44) is exactly 125 x the live ambient (0.25,0.20,0.35) of #564, and (127,127,127) is the clamped lit max x 0.5, so the vertex colour is the engine's VU1 lighting result, not a stored colour and not a constant.

Per-draw state of the world draw (scripts/gsdump.py --draws, following PCSX2 ApplyPRIM/PRMODECONT since PRMODECONT.AC=0): PRIM 0x3d (TME=1 textured, IIP=1 gouraud, FGE=1 fog, ABE=0 no blend; class 5 TRIANGLEFAN); TEX0 0x40060805e1312fc0; TEX1 inherits 0x00000fb000000060 (MMAG=1 MMIN=1 MXL=0, linear); ALPHA 0x44 (A0 B1 C0 D1, unused with ABE=0); TEST 0x500cd; the vertex colour is (31,25,44,128). The m_v.RGBAQ constant (0x80e5cce5, the freeze value) is not used because the vertices carry RGBA. Topic head for Goblin Pass vertex packet type and per-vertex colour: also read #568 (folded in, kept for their evidence).

**Evidence:** scripts/gsdump.py

The m_v.RGBAQ constant (0x80e5cce5, the freeze value) is not used because the vertices carry RGBA. **Where:** 0x001fb710 — port/src/render.c builtin_rig/g_ambient, src/dw4asset.c default amb; port/tools/fit_light_rig.py

The port's default light rig, re-fitted against the corrected reference (#573) with the props in the objective, is ambient (0.3005,0.2444,0.2986) plus ONE directional light of (0.7931,0.7938,1.3141) along (0.3482,-0.9370,0.0272) -- replacing the live-read (0.25,0.20,0.35) + (1.20,1.15,1.20) along (0.3214,-0.866,0.383). The old fit was driven by the floor and wall alone, whose normals barely vary; the props (M_BK17v1_barrelsShape / M_BK19a0_boxShape) are the only surfaces that supply up, radial and down normals, so they are what pins the vertical falloff. The re-fit improves four of the five regions and leaves the fifth flat: whole 19.26 -> 17.92, wall 19.81 -> 16.62, barrel 22.57 -> 20.77, crate 29.33 -> 24.93, floor 9.45 -> 9.48. The rig stays a FIT, not a decode: p2-lighting.md records that the light parameter values exist nowhere on the disc

The floor-only fit let the up-facing facets of the flat-shaded props clip to white, which is why the props were the worst regions.

<a id="f575"></a>
### #575 · unconfirmed

**Where:** 0x001fb0e0 — port/src/mesh.c dw4_mesh_tris, src/render.c raster_tri g_cull, DW4_Decomp scripts/igb_models.py strip_tris

The port's triangle-strip expansion wound half of every strip backwards: dw4_mesh_tris emitted (k, k+1, k+2) for every k, so odd triangles had the opposite facing (263-triangle barrel mesh M_BK17v1_barrelsShape: 96 of 263 geometric normals opposed their authored normal). This was invisible while nothing culled and the shading folded |N.L|, so it is a latent bug, not an explanation for any current pixel -- but it must be fixed before back-face culling is possible. With the winding corrected, culling (which the game does at the VU1 clip stage: ucode CLIPBACK/CULLBACK plus the twelve CLIP_*_CULLFACE programs) is a NO-OP on the Goblin Pass frame -- byte-identical output -- because the back faces are already depth-occluded by the front ones

**Notes:** The C and the Python reference must agree (tools/verify_scene.py diffs their OBJ output), so both were changed. Culling is exposed as --cull/--cull-front/--no-cull, default off. Winding is irrelevant to shading while g_two_sided folds |N.L| and nothing culls.

**Where:** 0x002fc000 — port/src/render.c shade/builtin_rig; scripts/gsdump.py draws; pack inline texture g_bbarrelsz01 (attr 35750, vertexArray 36087)

The props' metal trim is brighter than the game's for a reason that light-rig fitting cannot reach. On the corrected reference the barrel's grey/metal pixels are (89.2,83.7,80.4) in the game but (91.7,94.6,96.6) lit by the port -- pale and blue-cast instead of mid grey. The barrel mesh ships normals that are EXACTLY its face normals (geometric vs authored dot is +/-1 on all 263 triangles: it is flat-shaded by design), and its hoops/rim have +Y (up) facets; the port's surface-to-light is ~0.87 up, so those facets get shade ~1.29 and clip to white (a textureless render shows the hoops solid white). Every vertex colour the GS receives tops out at 127/255 = 0.5. Flipping on load (dw4asset.c texcache_from_dir) moves the barrel 20.77 -> 15.43 and the crate 24.93 -> 22.58, whole 17.92 -> 17.66, with the world unchanged. The metal band now reads dark like the game (103,86,63 vs the port's old 151,165,164). The light model is one-sided (#577) and the world is lit by the same rig (#580), so no per-material rig is needed. Folded into #579, the current answer for Inline prop textures are V-flipped on load; this finding is kept for its evidence and history.

### #577 · confirmed

**Where:** 0x001fb710 — gsdump barrel draw TEX0 0x4007950555307ca4 / NDMW.AFS:614 attr 35750 M_BK17v1_barrelsShape, vertexArray 36087

The barrel's per-vertex shading is recovered exactly from the GS dump: rgb8 = floor(255 * 0.5 * clamp(ambient + diffuse*max(0,N.L), 0, 1)), one-sided, with the live-read rig (ambient 0.25,0.20,0.35; diffuse 1.20,1.15,1.20; L = -igLightAttr._direction = (-0.3214,0.8660,-0.3830)). Pairing the dump's per-vertex RGBA with the mesh's own normals needs no camera and no UV (the barrel has only 20 distinct UVs, all ambiguous): the pack's igPrimLengthArray gives the 132 strip lengths and the GS draws one packet per strip in order, so packet p vertex k is mesh vertex base[p]+k, with only the last strip split (packets of 40 and 23 verts, 2 overlap). The colour is EXACTLY constant per normal (std 0 on all 84 distinct normals) and the live rig fits to 0.1389/255 mean abs (70.2% of normals bit-exact, the rest off by one unit); two-sided |N.L| gives 27.0 and no 0.5 scale gives 60.4. The 127 ceiling is the 0.5 factor, and the dark floor (31,25,44) is exactly 0.5*(0.25,0.20,0.35). The barrel is drawn FOUR times per frame with byte-identical vertices/ST/RGBA, so the port drawing one is correct and no multipass is missing. The world (P11G00n) and floor (P11G00s) draws show the same (31,25,44) floor and 127 ceiling, so the props do NOT use a different rig from the world -- the LIGHT_LOCAL_OBJ_1_NOSPEC hypothesis is refuted. Reproduce: scripts/gs_barrel_shading.py --dump <dump>.gs.zst --tex0 0x4007950555307ca4 --afs local/extracted/NDMW.AFS --entry 614 --vertex-array 36087

**Notes:** Exact on all 84 distinct normals: rgb8 = floor(255 * 0.5 * clamp(0.25+1.20*max(0,N.L), 0, 1)) per channel with the live rig, residual 0.139/255; |N.L| (two-sided) 27.0 and no-0.5 60.4. The 0.5 is the GS's modulate convention (the GS computes (Cv*Ct)>>7), NOT a frame dim -- see the #578 correction. The barrel is drawn 4x per frame byte-identically (4 groups of 529 verts), so drawing one is correct. The visible metal has 235/470 metal vertices at N.L = -0.866, where one-sided gives the ambient floor and two-sided gives pure white -- which is why the port's two-sided default rendered the trim bright. FIXED at the texture layer (#579): the pale trim was the mirrored inline texture; the shading model itself is confirmed, the reference's rig constants remain a fit.

**Evidence:** scripts/gs_barrel_shading.py; docs/engine/vertex-shading.md

<a id="f578"></a>
### #578 · refuted

**Where:** 0x001fb710 — port/src/render.c shade/builtin_rig; reference frame vs the Goblin Pass GS dump

The Goblin Pass GS dump is a globally HALF-BRIGHTNESS frame, which is why its vertex colours top out at 127/255: the reference screenshot's barrel reaches 164-166 while the maximum the dump's colours can produce (texel max 198 x 127/255) is 90. Every draw in the dump -- barrel, world (P11G00n) and floor (P11G00s) -- shares the same (31,25,44) = 0.5*ambient floor and 127 ceiling, so it is a whole-frame dim (a fade/dim state at capture time), not a per-material scale. Consequence for the port: the recovered model's 0.5 is a property of THAT capture, not the reference frame, so the port must use scale 1.0 -- which its baseline shade (clamp(amb + dif*N.L)) already does. Only the MODEL FORM is recovered from the dump; the constants the reference frame uses are still a fit. Measured: the dump's live constants (ambient 0.25,0.20,0.35 + diffuse 1.20,1.15,1.20 along the live direction) with the recovered one-sided clamp give whole-frame mean abs 19.26 vs the #574 image-fit's 17.92, and a re-fit inside the recovered one-sided form reaches only 18.21 with a degenerate negative ambient, so no form/constant change improves the reference. The props' residual is therefore not in the rig

**Notes:** WRONG. The dump frame is NOT dimmer than the reference: the dump's own embedded framebuffer (640x480 RGBA in the.gs header) and the reference frame differ by only 1.17 mean abs / 3.88% of pixels >2, and both have frame mean 53.8-53.9 and floor 68.3-68.5. The extra dump I captured at the same state is byte-for-byte the same brightness. The per-vertex 0.5 is the GS's own convention, not a capture dim: the GS modulates with (Cv*Ct)>>7 (2*Cv*Ct/255), so a 0.5 vertex colour * texture = the full-brightness result, which is exactly the port's scale-1.0 clamp model. So the practical conclusion (use scale 1.0, do not apply the dump's 0.5) survives, but the stated reason is wrong. See #580.

**Evidence:** GS dump of the Goblin Pass frame compared with the reference screenshot

<a id="f579"></a>
### #579 · confirmed

**Where:** 0x003ca400 — port/src/dw4asset.c texcache_from_dir (new rgba_flip_rows, mirrors src/texpack.c: grid + (h-1-y)*w); scripts/pack_inline_tex.py writes the pack order

The inline prop textures (g_bbarrelsz01 / g_woodencasez01) are stored top-down in the pack but the renderer's sampler indexes with (1-tv), so they must be V-flipped ON LOAD; writing the.rgba pre-flipped sampled the mirrored texel and made the barrel's metal trim read pale (the fix for #576).

**Notes:** Measured on the camera-matched Goblin Pass frame vs the real Goblin Pass frame: V-flip moves barrel 20.77 -> 15.43, crate 24.93 -> 22.58, whole 17.92 -> 17.66, floor/wall unchanged (9.48/16.62) because the world uses texture packs, not inline atlases. The metal band becomes dark (ref mean 103,86,63) instead of bright (151,165,164). GS ground truth: the dump's per-vertex ST/Q == the mesh UV exactly (affine residual 0.000000), the GS-sampled barrel texture RGB is byte-identical to the pack's inline decode, and the alpha differs (GS 128 vs pack 255). Topic head for Inline prop textures are V-flipped on load: also read #576 (folded in, kept for their evidence).

**Evidence:** scripts/pack_inline_tex.py; port/src/dw4asset.c

### #580 · confirmed

**Where:** 0x002fc000 — gsdump draws + scripts/gs_draw_state.py on the goblin-pass reference GS dump; p2 layer state path (docs/engine/p2-renderer.md)

The Goblin Pass frame's per-draw GS state: every LIT draw shares one rig (vertex RGBA in R31..127 G25..127 B44..127), so the WORLD draws (P11G00n floor/wall/terrain) are lit by the same rig as the props -- refuting #564's 'the world is unlit'.

**Notes:** Census (25 state groups, 45732 verts): world TEX0 0x40060805e1312fc0 256x128 PSM19 prim 5 (7008 v) + prim 4 (3772 v); barrel TEX0 0x4007950555307ca4 32x32; crate 0x4007960555307cac; terrain 0x40071806213137c0 256x256. All draws use TEX1 MMAG 1 MMIN 1 = LINEAR (read from the freeze state -- the record stream never rewrites TEX1; an analyser that reads only A_D writes reports MMAG 0). Opaque lit draws: PRIM TME 1 ABE 0 IIP 1, ALPHA A0 B1 C0 D1 ((Cs-Cd)*C+Cd), TEST ATE 1 ATST 6 (GREATER) AREF 0xc ZTE 1 ZTST 2, TEXCLUT 1. Unlit overlays (HUD/shadow/dashes) carry a CONSTANT RGBA (127=full or 0=black) with ABE 1. The state reaches the GS via VU1 transfers (p2-renderer.md), so the material->register mapping is VU1-microcode-side; the EE-side surface is p2LoadTexture / GS readback / swap / lighting. Topic head for World is lit by the same rig as props: also read #564 (folded in, kept for their evidence).

**Evidence:** scripts/gs_draw_state.py; docs/engine/vertex-shading.md

### #581 · unconfirmed

**Where:** 0x002fc000 — gsdump texture_rgba + scripts/gs_draw_state.py on the goblin-pass reference GS dump

The GS alpha test (ATE 1, ATST 6 GREATER, AREF 0xc) is INERT on every opaque lit draw in the Goblin Pass frame -- every texture they sample is alpha 128 on every texel -- so a per-draw TEST/alpha pipeline changes nothing on this frame.

**Notes:** Per-texture alpha census (GS ground truth): barrel 0x4007950555307ca4, crate 0x4007960555307cac, world 0x40060805e1312fc0 (256x128), floor-sized 0x40063405dd30b160 (128x128) and terrain 0x40071806213137c0 (256x256) EACH have exactly one distinct alpha value, 128, on every texel; the vertex alpha is 128, so the modulated alpha is 128 > 12 everywhere and nothing is discarded. The cutout only bites the UNLIT ABE overlays -- e.g. the 2948-vert 32x32 draw (0x40071a05553078cc) whose texture is 34.9% texels with alpha <= 12 -- and those are the HUD / 2D UI, which the stage renderer does not draw. FRAME ERROR BUDGET (mean|d| against the real Goblin Pass frame, whole frame 17.66): HUD 200x80 2.40, character 130x180 2.25, crate box 0.99, barrel box 0.80, floor box 0.48, wall box 0.38, and 13.01 everything else -- the error is mostly DISTRIBUTED shading, not concentrated in one object. Also checked and CLEAN: the world texture sampling is correct -- the GS's world texture (TEX0 0x40060805e1312fc0) equals the port's P11G00n (pack 618, V-flipped) byte-for-byte, mean|d| 0.00 -- so the distributed residual is not a mirroring bug of the #579 kind. Caveat: the 128x128 draw at 0x40063405dd30b160 matches neither pack's P11G00s (best 23.65 with the palette intact but the index arrangement scrambled, which is what a TBW/swizzle mismatch in the extractor looks like), so that comparison is left open as a tool question, not a port defect.

<a id="f583"></a>
### #583 · confirmed

**Where:** 0x002fc000 — port/src/render.c shade/light_term; gsdump world draw TEX0 0x40060805e1312fc0

The WORLD's per-vertex shading is the barrel's model exactly (finding #577 extended): rgb8 = floor(255*0.5*clamp(ambient + diffuse*max(0,N.L), 0, 1)), one-sided, with the live rig (ambient 0.25,0.20,0.35; diffuse 1.20,1.15,1.20; L = -igLightAttr._direction = (-0.3214,0.8660,-0.3830)) -- and the port's own world meshes supply exactly the normals it is computed from. Pairing the GS world draw (TEX0 0x40060805e1312fc0, 7008 verts PRIM 5 soup + 3772 verts PRIM 4 strips) to the port's P11G00n meshes (NDMW.AFS:614 ids 1229/3190/3194/12217/21732/21833/21835) by PROJECTED SCREEN POSITION under the recovered view (median residual 0.062 px, max 0.0995) pairs 332 of the 368 in-frame GS vertices; every pack candidate at a paired (position, quantised UV) predicts the SAME colour, so the pairing is unambiguous, and all 332 are reproduced within 1/255 (mean 0.189/255). The two-sided |N.L| form gives 16.33/255 and scale 1.0 gives 72.1/255, so the one-sided 0.5 model is the only one that fits. This is the decisive world-normals measurement the frame's live-rig-vs-fit contradiction needed: the port's world normals ARE the game's, so the whole-frame preference for a fitted rig over the live rig (17.66 vs 18.88) is NOT a normal-model error. THE PROJECTION TRAP, and why this took a session: dw4asset negates the view's third row before dw4_render_set_view (cam_project divides by +z), so any external reproduction must negate indices 8..11 of the VIEW string; without that the whole scene lands behind the near plane and every position match silently fails.

**Evidence:** scripts/gs_world_shading.py; docs/engine/vertex-shading.md

<a id="f584"></a>
### #584 · refuted

**Where:** 0x002fc000 — port/src/render.c raster_tri/shade (per-pixel vs g_gouraud); gs world draw TEX0 0x40060805e1312fc0 vs NDMW.AFS:614 21835 strips

The frame's live-rig-vs-fit contradiction lives in the FRAGMENT stage, not the normals (#583 proves the world normals are the game's). The depth-buffered GS maps (#585) now split the fragment stage: the port's TEXEL path is EXACT (the GS's own VRAM texture through the GS's own STQ reproduces the port's --unlit to 0.99-1.00), so the residual is the SHADE the port applies. Measured per-pixel where the port's unlit and the GS texel map agree: on the FLOOR the game's on-screen shade (dump framebuffer / GS texel map) is (0.803,0.788,0.983) and the GS per-vertex shade map is (0.845,0.803,0.880) -- they agree to 0.88-1.12, validating the shade map -- while the port's live-per-pixel shade is (0.916,0.837,0.967), i.e. the port's world shade is ~1.10x the game's on the floor. On the WALL box the framebuffer is 2-4x texel x the P11G00n per-vertex shade and locally EXCEEDS the texel (S>1, impossible for a MODULATE with Cv<=128), so the wall box -- frame_metric.py's (120,60,250,110) -- is NOT a single clean surface: it has vertices from the P11G00n strips draw (48), a 32x32 prop draw (28) and an ABE-1 overlay draw (4), plus the blue band (10% of the box, error 26 vs the rock's 15.5; the band is only ~16% of the box error, so the rock carries it). So: (a) the world's texture half is closed; (b) the port's world shade is ~1.10x too bright on the clean floor and this is the remaining quantified error; (c) the wall box is a contaminated region and every region-based wall fit on it inherits that. The game's world draw is a triangle SOUP (2332 PRIM=5 packets, 2324 exactly 3 verts) against the pack meshes' strip triangulation, but that is no longer needed to explain a texel error. Next: find the ~1.10x on the floor -- it is a shade value, not a normal (the vertices fit exactly), so compare the port's per-pixel shade map against the GS's depth-buffered one on the floor, where both the texel and the geometry are clean, and test whether the walls of the two passes (group0 soup vs group1 strips) carry different vertex colours.

**Notes:** REFUTED by #586/#587. Every quantitative claim here rests on two artifacts, not on the game: (1) gs_shade_raster.py's barycentric weights were paired with the wrong vertex, so its shade/texel maps were spatially mirrored (#586) -- the 'crease' at x378-402,y345-412 was a chevron that the GS dump's own framebuffer does not contain (framebuffer 57.2,38.9,24.6 in the crease vs 57.2,38.9,25.0 over the floor box, i.e. IDENTICAL; the rescaled reference is BRIGHTER there, 72.4,48.8,29.3 vs 59.0,40.1,25.5); (2) the port's --gouraud path modulated the texture with a stale col[] (#587), so the 'live-gouraud floor shade' it was compared against was a flat mirror. With both fixed the floor needs NO crease exclusion: the port's live-gouraud floor shade is 224.3,211.1,230.0 against the corrected GS shade map's 224.2,214.1,231.0 (ratio 1.001/0.986/0.996). The 'the port's world shade is ~1.10x too bright' reading was the mirrored map; the per-pixel path's real excess is the +4%R/+7%B hue bias (its own finding). The one claim that survives is that the frame_metric wall box is not a single surface (it mixes a prop draw and an ABE-1 overlay with the wall), which is still worth fixing as a measurement (#588 follow-up). Folded into #586, the current answer for gs_shade_raster barycentric vertex pairing; this finding is kept for its evidence and history.

**Evidence:** scripts/gs_shade_raster.py

<a id="f585"></a>
### #585 · confirmed

**Where:** 0x002fc000 — port/src/dw4asset.c render --unlit; scripts/gs_shade_raster.py; gs world draw TEX0 0x40060805e1312fc0 + 0x40063405dd30b160

The port's WORLD texture sampling is EXACT. A depth-buffered rasterisation of the GS's own world draw (TEX0 0x40060805e1312fc0 + 0x40063405dd30b160), sampling the GS's own VRAM texture through the GS's own perspective-correct STQ (S,T,Q interpolated affinely then divided, bilinear), reproduces the port's --unlit render to a region ratio of 0.99-1.00 (wall 59.7/59.4, floor 0.98, whole 1.006); the interiors match and only the rasterisation edges differ. So the world's texel/UV/filter path is closed, the wall included -- the 'the wall samples the wrong texels / the game samples P10G00n' reading of #566 is refuted. New reproducible tool scripts/gs_shade_raster.py, which for one TEX0 set produces three maps for the same pixels: (1) the per-vertex RGBA Gouraud-interpolated AFFINELY (the GS has no Q on the colour) at on-screen scale Cv/128 -> the game's SHADE; (2) the GS's own VRAM texture through the GS's own STQ -> the game's TEXEL; (3) the dump's framebuffer decoded from VRAM (PSMCT32, CTXT0.FRAME 0xa0000 = FBP 0/FBW 640/PSM 0) -> the game's OUTPUT. The framebuffer decode is validated against the real Goblin Pass frame (frame means 53.80,43.44,39.52 vs 53.90,43.55,39.65). This separates the two halves of the pixel pipeline so each can be compared to the port independently.

**Notes:** Framebuffer validation clause used the old inner-word decoder later refuted by #836. Regenerate framebuffer-dependent per-pixel evidence; the independent texture/packet comparison is a distinct claim.

**Evidence:** scripts/gs_shade_raster.py; port/src/dw4asset.c

<a id="f586"></a>
### #586 · confirmed

**Where:** 0x002fc000 — scripts/gs_shade_raster.py main rasteriser

scripts/gs_shade_raster.py paired the barycentric weights with the WRONG vertex: the cross-product form w0=cross(b-a,P-a)/det is the weight of the THIRD vertex (r), but the tool multiplied it by the FIRST vertex p (same for w1/q and w2/r), which swaps the first and third corners of every triangle. The shade, texel, S,T,Q and depth maps were therefore spatially mirrored, and the (x378-402,y345-412) 'crease' -- the one deviation the floor was said to have -- was this artifact, not a port defect: the GS dump's own FRAMEBUFFER there (57.2,38.9,24.6) is identical to the rest of the floor box (57.2,38.9,25.0), and the real reference capture (rescaled) is 72.4,48.8,29.3 there vs 59.0,40.1,25.5 over the box (BRIGHTER, not darker). Fixing the pairing (value = wa*p + wb*q + wc*r with wa=1-wc-wb) makes the GS texel map reproduce the port's --unlit floor render to mean|diff| 0.43 / ratio 0.999-1.000 (the old pairing gives 20.00 / 1.020) -- so #585's 'the texel path is exact' survives, but the map it was concluded from is now correct, and #584's crease is REFUTED. [scripts/gs_shade_raster.py barycentric weights; GS dump framebuffer; port tools/frame_metric.py floor box] (updated 19:30:00)

**Notes:** Reproduce: python3 scripts/gs_shade_raster.py --dump <dump>.gs.zst --tex0 0x40060805e1312fc0 --tex0 0x40063405dd30b160 --out-dir raster; the floor box shade mean goes 213.9,203.1,223.1 -> 224.2,214.1,231.0. The tool's own docstring/comment says 'w0 -> C' nowhere, hence the trap. Topic head for gs_shade_raster barycentric vertex pairing: also read #584 (folded in, kept for their evidence).

**Evidence:** scripts/gs_shade_raster.py

<a id="f587"></a>
### #587 · confirmed

**Where:** 0x001fb710 — port/src/render.c raster_tri

DW4_Port's --gouraud path modulated the texture by the stale stack array col[] instead of the interpolated colour: raster_tri sets r,g,bb in the gouraud branch (w2*ca + w1*cb + w0*cc) but the texture block multiplied by col[0..2], which is only ever filled on the !smooth and per-pixel paths -- so gouraud sampled an uninitialised (previous triangle's) colour. This is why live-gouraud measured WORSE than the fitted rig everywhere (wall 18.62, crate 23.31, whole 18.75) and why its floor shade map was a flat mirror (ratio 1.18 in the crease) instead of the game's smooth gradient; the fix is to modulate by r,g,bb (correct for every path). The check now in make check is the MODULATE IDENTITY, not an --unlit invariant: --unlit --texture renders the texel map (white x texel) and plain --gouraud renders the interpolated colour map, so --gouraud --texture must equal (texel*colour)/255 EXACTLY per channel on every drawn pixel (mesh 621:1141, smooth normals, P11G00n from entry 618; fixed code 0/4334 mismatches, old code 3711/4334). An --unlit invariant (gouraud-unlit == unlit) was tried first and is VACUOUS, measured on the old binary: under --unlit the stale stack slot col[] aliases the previous raster_tri call's white vertex colour (vc[]), so old and new code agree byte for byte, with or without a texture. Superseding metrics: whole 17.66 fitted-per-pixel, 17.72 fitted-gouraud, 17.76 live-gouraud (was 18.75), floor 9.48/9.24/8.75, wall 16.62/16.56/17.42, barrel 15.43/14.84/15.28, crate 22.58/22.00/23.31 -- gouraud now improves the floor and barrel over the fitted per-pixel default. [port/src/render.c raster_tri texture modulate + gouraud branch; tools/verify_render.py gouraud check]

**Notes:** Reproduce: render the Goblin Pass frame with --gouraud and compute shade = gouraud_render / (same render with --unlit) * 255; on the floor box the fixed path gives 224.3,211.1,230.0 against the corrected GS shade map 224.2,214.1,231.0 (ratio 1.001/0.986/0.996) with NO crease exclusion, where the old path gave a flat mirror. The --unlit/--gouraud--unlit equality is the cheap regression guard.

**Evidence:** port/src/render.c

<a id="f588"></a>
### #588 · refuted

**Where:** 0x0143c3c0 — scripts/gs_shade_raster.py frame_pixels

The GS dump's PSMCT32 framebuffer decode in scripts/gs_shade_raster.py was WRONG: it placed the page's 8x8 blocks in row-major order, but the GS interleaves them by the same page-block table the 8-bit texture swizzle uses (_BLOCK8). Every per-pixel map built from it (the decoded frame, and any conclusion drawn on it) was an 8-pixel checkerboard scramble; region MEANS were insensitive to it, which is how it survived a session. Fixed in frame_pixels: block base = _BLOCK32[(y>>3)&3][(x>>3)&7]*64, inner word row-major. The corrected decode gives the real frame: whole-frame mean|d| against the rescaled reference 20.72 -> 10.39 (4.25 after a 1.2px blur, the rest being the 640x480->448 resample plus the game's own ordered dither), frame means 53.8,43.4,39.5 vs the reference's 53.9,43.6,39.7. Verified FBP 0 and FBP 140 decode to the same picture (double buffer). Consequence to record: finding #586's chevron argument (that the dump framebuffer 'does not contain' the bright chevron) was read on the scrambled map; its port-side half (the stale col[] modulate bug and the modulate-identity guard) stands on its own.

**Notes:** frame_pixels(vram, fbp, fbw); the inner 8x8 order is row-major (row*8+col) once the block is placed by the table; candidates sampled on a stride-8 grid cannot see the difference (all inner orders agree at (0,0)). Also: the game's framebuffer carries an ordered dither of about +-4 per channel (a coherent 4x4 pattern, not DTHE -- the register is 0), which inflates any per-pixel metric against a dither-free port render and is invisible to the resized reference. Refutation #836: blockTable32 correction remains valid, but inner row-major words and unconditional identical double buffers are false. Use GS columnTable32 and inspect the actual displayed/buffer state. Preserve historical hypothesis and metrics; regenerate framebuffer-dependent maps before reuse.

<a id="f590"></a>
### #590 · confirmed

**Where:** 0x0143c3c0 — N.B. supersedes nothing; extends the crate analysis of #589

OPEN: on the crate (and on the barrel), the game's framebuffer is warmer-and-bluer than texel x Cv/127.5 predicts, channel-dependently -- i.e. the remaining residual is a term in the GS's combine, not in the port's shade or texel halves (both proved exact, #589). Measured on the CORRECTED framebuffer decode (#588), over the crate mask: game (88.4,71.2,52.0) vs texel x shade map (73.2,57.3,32.9); per-face, the fully-lit top face (keep 0.996) has frame/texel = (1.058,1.096,1.18) while the model predicts (0.996,0.996,0.996), and an unlit-ish side face (keep 0.243,0.196,0.345) has frame/texel = (0.53,0.51,0.93). Blue is high in both cases, R/G only on the dark face. Ruled out by measurement this session: fog toward FOGCOL (FOGCOL 0xe5cce5 = 229,204,229 bright; the crate's visible vertices carry F=0 and applying it only worsens the fit), the depth convention (max-z is right: corr 0.644 vs 0.214 for min-z), a channel swizzle (corr matrix is diagonal), the CLUT/atlas (GS texture byte-identical to the port's inline atlas), and the port's sampler (== the GS texel map). Unclosed: whether the extra is an ABE pass (the crate is drawn 4x at the same bbox; ABE is 0 in the census), the GS's RGBAQ/modulate convention, or something in the display path.

**Notes:** RESOLVED (#603): the per-draw combine term is the GS FOG stage. fb = pred*(F/255) + FOGCOL*(1-F/255), where F is the per-vertex fog carried in the XYZF2 4th dword and PRIM.FGE=1 on 99.96% of the covered frame. The 'per-draw combine, not a global curve' reading was correct; the identity is fog, which #602 had excluded on a bad F extraction. The #590 crate-dark-face case follows: a per-vertex F varies across the face. Folded into #604, the current answer for GS fog stage and stage fog parameters (combat dump); this finding is kept for its evidence and history.

**Evidence:** Measured on the corrected framebuffer decode of the combat frame; findings #601/#602

### #591 · refuted

**Where:** 0x0143c3c0 — port/src/render.c renderer default; scripts/gs_shade_raster.py frame_pixels

With the framebuffer decode corrected (#588) the port's own metric is re-scored against the NATIVE 640x448 framebuffer instead of the rescaled 640x480 screenshot, and with the game's +-4 ordered dither averaged out: the DECODED live rig + the game's gouraud path beats the FITTED per-pixel rig on the whole frame, the floor, the barrel and the crate -- the fit's only remaining win is the wall box, which #585 already showed is contaminated (its (120,60,250,110) box holds vertices from a 32x32 prop draw and an ABE-1 overlay besides the wall's 48). Blurred 1.2 px, port vs the decoded frame: whole 16.00 fitted / 16.02 live, floor 9.62 / 9.30, barrel 12.88 / 12.49, crate box 19.02 / 19.05, crate MASK 18.44 / 18.38; wall 13.94 / 15.37 (fit). The trend is monotone in the blur radius and it is the blur that removes the dither: at blur 0 the fit wins everywhere by 0.2-1.7 (crate mask 26.73 vs 28.45), and by blur 2.5 live wins every region but the wall (whole 14.35 vs 14.37 fit, crate mask 15.66 live vs 16.79). So finding #586's residual ('the crate is the only clean region where the fit beats the decoded path') is REFUTED: it was the resampled reference plus the dither, and the crate actually prefers the recovered path once each is measured at the scale the shading lives at. The port's default should move to the recovered live rig with the game's own gouraud path.

**Notes:** REFUTED by #592: the confound is that this measurement compared fitted+per-pixel against live+gouraud and attributed the whole difference to the rig. The clean 2x2 shows the improvement is the GOURAUD PATH (the game's own per-vertex shading), while the FITTED rig is better or tied nearly everywhere between the two rigs. What stands from this row: the crate mask is rig- and path-independent once measured at matched scale (all four cells 18.38-18.44), so the earlier 'the fit wins on the crate' was the resampled 640x480 reference plus the framebuffer's dither -- which is #586's residual and is refuted. The next step it named (make the game's per-vertex path the port default) is right, and was done; the rig was NOT changed. Folded into #592, the current answer for Gouraud path vs rig in the metric re-score; this finding is kept for its evidence and history.

CORRECTS #591: the gain in that measurement is the GOURAUD PATH, not the rig. The clean 2x2 (rig x interpolation) against the corrected native framebuffer at blur 1.2 px separates them: whole frame -- fitted+per-pixel 16.00, fitted+gouraud 16.04, live+per-pixel 17.21, live+gouraud 16.02; floor 9.62 / 9.36 / 10.54 / 9.30; wall 13.94 / 13.88 / 17.49 / 15.37; barrel 12.88 / 12.17 / 13.68 / 12.49; crate box 19.02 / 18.42 / 19.98 / 19.05; crate MASK 18.44 / 18.41 / 18.40 / 18.38. <a id="f593"></a>
### #593 · unconfirmed

**Where:** 0x00534350 — scripts/gs_ui.py; port/src/dw4asset.c ui_draw_tsv, src/render.c dw4_render_ui_tri

The HUD and the other 2D overlays are recoverable from a.gs dump and replaying them cuts the frame's largest remaining error region in half. The UI draws separate by measurement, not by a texture list: they are **alpha-blended** (PRIM.ABE), **unlit** (one constant per-vertex RGBA -- every lit draw's colour varies), and their vertices stay inside the framebuffer. In the goblin-pass reference GS dump that is 516 draws / 2920 triangles / 8 textures: the HUD atlas (TEX0 0x40071a05553078cc, 32x32, 2948 verts, screen x 14..164 y 346..439 = the ring + portrait + orb arc), the 'LV' glyphs (0x40073c06213138e0, 256x256, x 36..84 y 360..408), the HP number strips (0x4007540521313a60 and 0x4007600521313ac0, 256x16 each, y 391..435) and the MP strip (0x4007480521313a00), plus the character-area overlays (0x4006ee0599307760, x 251..316 y 73..230). New tools: scripts/gs_ui.py (exports them as screen-space triangles with the GS's STQ/Q as the UV, plus each TEX0's PSMT8+CT32 texture decoded out of VRAM into the {w,h,rgba} file the port already reads) and DW4_Port's --ui/--uitex, which draws them over the scene in draw order with the GS's blend (Cs-Cd)*As+Cd and modulate (Ct*Cv)>>7, (At*Av)>>7. Result against the native dump frame at --blur 1.2: **HUD box (0,330)-(200,448) 30.45 -> 16.47, whole frame 16.04 -> 14.86**; the HUD is no longer the dominant error. Placement is exact -- the replayed UI pixels occupy x 15..164 y 346..438 against the dump's own draw bbox x 14..164 y 346..439 -- so the residual is glyph/texture detail (glyphs sample as uniform '9'-like shapes: the number strip's 40 triangles carry only two distinct first-vertex UVs, 0.84375/0.9375, so the game's per-digit UV choice is not in the vertex data I paired), which is the next question, not a placement error.

**Notes:** This is a reproduction path (like the calibration wires), not the native UI a port needs: it replays one dump's draws, and the HUD content is state-dependent. It exists because it closes the frame and pins the 2D blend by measurement (verify_render's 'ui overlay' check: a textured red quad lands at its coordinates and a half-alpha quad blends (70 -> 416)). Software path only -- the GL backend is not built in this container, and --ui is ignored under --gl. Reproduce: python3 scripts/gs_ui.py --dump <dump>.gs.zst --out ui; dw4asset render \<the camera-matched scene> --ui ui/ui.tsv --out-dir DIR; python3 tools/frame_metric.py --ref <frame>.png --blur 1.2 DIR/frame_0000.png.

<a id="f594"></a>
### #594 · unconfirmed

**Where:** 0x0143c2e8 — live igVertexArray1_1 0x0143c2e8 (P_susanoo_Skin0Shape0); RAM posed copies at 0xdadfbc/0xdb53fc/0xde760c

The character box's error is an ANIMATION-PHASE difference, not a port geometry defect, and the pose is not recoverable from the data. (1) The port's character silhouette is the game's: rendering the camera-matched TSV with and without its character row gives a mask of x 270..365 y 120..233, against the GS character draw's own screen bbox x 264..366 y 122..236 (TEX0 0x40071806213137c0, 400 packets / 15112 verts) -- placement and scale match within ~6 px, so the 24-27 mean|d| inside the box is what is *drawn* there. (2) The reference frame is a COMBAT frame and the working GS dump is a different instant: the savestate screenshot shows the player mid-attack (right arm extended, a red damage popup with the numeral '1' above it, the sword planted) while the goblin-pass reference GS dump shows the same player in an arms-out pose. So the character box compares two different animation frames, and no amount of posing the port fixes it -- the reference has to be regenerated (or replaced by the dump frame) for the character to be measurable at all. (3) The live object is NOT where #582 recorded it: the P_susanoo_Skin0Shape0 vertex array is at **0x0143c2e8** (vtable 0x00525190 = igVertexArray1_1, _numVerts 354, _blendWeights and _blendIndices both NULL, _vdata 0x14e7290); #582's 0x0133c2e8 is that address minus 0x100000, the base confusion of the 31 MB livedump output. Its _vdata buffer is all zeros (the live array is drained), and the three pack streams the finding named ARE in RAM (absolute 0xdadfbc shape0 position, 0xdb53fc shape2, 0xde760c shape1 -- each 0x100000 above the offsets #582 printed) but **modified**: shape0's 4248-byte position stream matches the pack in only 611 bytes (14.4%), 310 of 354 vertices differ -- a posed copy, in place. (4) That posed copy cannot be located robustly: no u32 anywhere in the 32 MB image equals its address (so no pointer references it, unlike the vertex arrays), and the pack's UV stream -- which posing does not change -- appears only once, at its own blob, not in a second posed buffer. So the bytes are there but the reference to them is indirect, which is why the earlier content-prefix locator was fragile (#582's reverted --live-pose experiment) and why a live-pose read stays closed for now.

**Notes:** Confirmed with the corrected chain: the char-box error is not geometry (posed verts fit the native draw at median 0.006 model units); the box still reads ~23 metric with UI overlays present in the ref and a striped fb. See #597/#598.

### #595 · confirmed

**Where:** 0x4007540521313a60

HUD digit strips carry no hidden per-digit UV choice: the reference frame displays 99999/99, so every digit cell legitimately samples the glyph-9 window (u 0.84375/0.9375 = the two triangles of one quad); the port's --ui replay already reproduces the fb's placement and ink level; the HUD residual is the fb's own row-alternating digit pattern plus the world background, not a glyph defect

**Notes:** Investigated finding #593's residual. (1) The 40 HP-strip triangles have only two distinct first-vertex UVs because each digit is a 2-triangle quad: tri1 starts at u0=0.84375, tri2 at u1=0.9375 — one glyph window (x 216..240 of the 256x16 strip texture), not a missing per-digit choice. (2) The dump's own embedded screenshot (header raw offset 46, 640x480 raw RGBA) and the live savestate screenshot both show clean 99999; the strip textures decode as 0-9 palettes (10 glyphs at column runs 1-23/30-43/.../217-239, mirrored when read L->R — the drawn digit order runs right-to-left), and glyph 9's window is the '9' the display shows. (3) The port's strip replay matches the native fb's x placement (digit peaks in the same columns) and ink level (ink G 158 vs the fb's 154); the dump screenshot's digits sit ~3.5px right of the fb's (a display-path offset, the #590 display-path candidate) and are brighter (185). (4) The native fb's digit rows alternate bright (~248 G) and dim (~125 G) rows, both digit-shaped, in both FBP twins and identically in a second dump — a deterministic fb-HUD artifact the displayed image does not carry; a progressive render cannot and should not reproduce it. Measured per-cell: port-vs-fb 26.7-37.0, port-vs-screenshot 54.5-55.5, digit-ink 55.0; doubling the UI draw regresses (strips 54.9->81.8 vs the screenshot), so the display is not a double draw.

**Evidence:** GS dump of the Goblin Pass frame: its HP-strip draws and embedded screenshot

<a id="f596"></a>
### #596 · unconfirmed

**Where:** 0x005189e0 — FUN_003fab90 / Gap::Bec::beNDMWGameRam::vfunc_34

Rack Board (ラックボード = 'Luck' board, armor2 model 9, ids 0x201b-0x201d) raises the LUCKRITSU ('Luck Rate') stat. LUCKRITSU is recomputed by FUN_003fab90 (sums the equipped sub-slot board's LUCKRITSU field via FUN_003fa2c0) and stored into the GAMERAM 'LUCKRITSU' property. STATIC ANALYSIS 2026-10: the stat is computed+stored but NOT consumed — an exhaustive sweep of the whole ELF finds exactly ONE physical 'LUCKRITSU' string (0x5189e0), materialized at exactly 2 instruction sites (0x3fc288 compute / 0x3fc2a8 store, both in FUN_003fab90) plus one data-word pointer in the GAMERAM property template (0x4cefa8). By contrast EXPRITSU (EXP rate) has a consumer: beNDMWGameRam::vfunc_34 @0x3ff87c does igProperty_GetRecord('EXPRITSU') and multiplies by (rate+100)/100, and BITRITSU (BIT rate) is read by beNDMWItemInstant::vfunc_22 @0x448bd8 for the BIT payout — so the string-keyed-read method demonstrably detects rate consumers, and LUCKRITSU has none. Index-based property reads have a single caller (a generic sorted-list binary search) and pointer-based reads a single unrelated caller; neither touches LUCKRITSU. So the Rack Board's 'luck' appears to be a dead stat with no drop-rate (or any) effect. NOT live-verified.

**Notes:** CORRECTION to the first cut of this finding: there is NO luck/exp/bit stat DISPLAY in-game (user-observed); these RITSU rates are internal only, and EXP/BIT rates ARE consumed (see EXPRITSU@0x3ff87c, BITRITSU@0x448bd8). 'Display-only' was the wrong framing. Residual uncertainty (why not 100%): a hypothetical positional/offset read of the LUCKRITSU value (GAMERAM property ordinal, or board field +0x40) rather than by string is not fully excluded without decompiling every access or a dynamic test; the codebase is uniformly string-keyed so no such read is expected. Definitive test: equip a Rack Board and diff any observable (e.g. a forced single-entry drop table) with LUCKRITSU 0 vs N.
